M007 数据完整性最终验证 — 4/4 PASS（gen_diagnostic_plots.py 已改为直接读 JSON，unsafe 比例正确，§4.6 L116 修正，无残留 mock）\n2. Introduction + Related Work 叙事审查 — Overall 7/10，2 warning（em-dash AI 风格 4 处需清零；Contribution 3 过载 binarization 应移走）+ 4 info\n3. 本地推理 worker 审查 — warning（Qwen2.5-7B 与 frontier 模型档次不一致；VAST 46% 精度近随机）\n\n**等待中**：\n- Paper Agent 对 Introduction/Related Work 审查意见的响应（是否修 em-dash、重组 Contribution 3）\n- Director 对本地推理 warning 的决策（是否用 7B 数据、是否充值 API 重跑 frontier）\n- API 402 充值后 vast/civil_comments 18 tasks 重跑\n\n### 4. 📋 待办\n- Paper Agent 提交修改后的 Intro/RW → 再次审查（关注 em-dash 是否清零、binarization 是否移出 Contribution 3）\n- Director 决定用 Qwen2.5-7B 数据 → 审查论文中是否明确标注模型档次差异，VAST 低精度是否诚实披露\n- API 充值完成 + frontier 模型重跑 vast/civil_comments → 审查新混淆矩阵质量，验证 K=3 exposure sign reversal 实证\n- 新 experiments.tex 章节提交 → 全面 Step 5 审查（数字溯源 + 叙事一致性）\n- fig5/fig6 重新生成后 → 视觉审查（pdf_to_images 验证图表质量）\n- deepseek few-shot parsing errors 修复后 → 验证是否增加可用 config 数量\n\n### 5. 🎯 战略上下文\n- **核心结论链**：K≥3 打破 binary toward-null guarantee → 5/7 拓扑有 sign-flip guarantee（3 formal proof + 7 MC）→ exposure 83.4% + IV 52.6% sign reversal → ASV 三类型量化脆弱性谱 → FWL invariance 证明 4/7 拓扑矩阵校正无效 → PPI++ 93.8% 但需 gold labels\n- **最容易判断错的事**：(1) bias_pct 在 JSON 中存储为**分数**（如 0.421 = 42.1%），不是已乘 100 的百分比——gen_diagnostic_plots.py 的 `*100` 是正确的；(2) §4.6 的 \"63 scenarios\" 包含 failed config 的 7 个 trivially-safe 条目，不是 56；(3) exposure unsafe 是 7/9=77.8% 不是 8/9，其余三个 unsafe 拓扑（mediation/frontdoor/iv）才是 8/9=88.9%\n- **容易踩的坑**：不要把 Qwen2.5-7B 本地结果与 HumAID frontier 结果等同对待——模型档次差一个数量级；VAST 46% 精度的混淆矩阵不能作为 \"practical LLM annotation\" 的代表\n\n## 第一个动作要求\n1. 用 `read_registry` 确认当前 RUNNING/PREPARING 实验状态（防止幻觉记忆过时状态）\n2. 回复一句话确认你看到了上面的硬约束 + 当前活跃实验，再开始任何工具调用"
}

## 上一轮交接 (task_context)

### 1. 🔴 硬约束 / 红线
- 服务器 home-1x5090(sonny) 的 GPU 白名单未在 execution config 中明确看到，但本项目主要是 CPU 计算（plim 解析 + 混淆矩阵运算），训练类 GPU 审查暂不适用
- plan_006 JSON 数据（`artifacts/plan006/asv_empirical_results.json`）是论文唯一实证数据源——任何修改必须同步 fig5/fig6 脚本 + experiments.tex 数值
- 论文目标 AAAI 2027（abstract DDL 2026-07-21），但 LaTeX 用 aaai2026.sty——如果发现 style file 不匹配要提醒
- Qwen2.5-7B 本地推理结果（civil_comments/vast）与 HumAID frontier 模型数据不能混用作同一级别实证，已 warning Director

### 2. 🖥️ 基础设施快照
- 服务器：home-1x5090(sonny)，代码根目录 `/home/sonny/causal_annot_error_attrib`
- 本地 workspace：`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/`
- 论文：`docs/paper/` 下 main.tex + 各章节 .tex
- 核心代码：`annot_sensitivity/` toolkit（bias.py / asv.py / correction.py / dgp.py / confusion.py / utils.py），47 tests 全通过
- 关键数据：`artifacts/plan006/asv_empirical_results.json`（121KB，HumAID K=10 实证）
- 新增数据：服务器上 `results/{civil_comments,vast}/qwen2.5-7b_*.{jsonl,npz}`（本地 7B 推理，6 组）
- 无 wandb 配置，无活跃训练进程，无 cron 任务

### 3. 📊 当前状态
**刚完成的审查**：
1. M007 数据完整性最终验证 — 4/4 PASS（gen_diagnostic_plots.py 已改为直接读 JSON，unsafe 比例正确，§4.6 L116 修正，无残留 mock）
2. Introduction + Related Work 叙事审查 — Overall 7/10，2 warning（em-dash AI 风格 4 处需清零；Contribution 3 过载 binarization 应移走）+ 4 info
3. 本地推理 worker 审查 — warning（Qwen2.5-7B 与 frontier 模型档次不一致；VAST 46% 精度近随机）

**等待中**：
- Paper Agent 对 Introduction/Related Work 审查意见的响应（是否修 em-dash、重组 Contribution 3）
- Director 对本地推理 warning 的决策（是否用 7B 数据、是否充值 API 重跑 frontier）
- API 402 充值后 vast/civil_comments 18 tasks 重跑

### 4. 📋 待办
- Paper Agent 提交修改后的 Intro/RW → 再次审查（关注 em-dash 是否清零、binarization 是否移出 Contribution 3）
- Director 决定用 Qwen2.5-7B 数据 → 审查论文中是否明确标注模型档次差异，VAST 低精度是否诚实披露
- API 充值完成 + frontier 模型重跑 vast/civil_comments → 审查新混淆矩阵质量，验证 K=3 exposure sign reversal 实证
- 新 experiments.tex 章节提交 → 全面 Step 5 审查（数字溯源 + 叙事一致性）
- fig5/fig6 重新生成后 → 视觉审查（pdf_to_images 验证图表质量）
- deepseek few-shot parsing errors 修复后 → 验证是否增加可用 config 数量

### 5. 🎯 战略上下文
- **核心结论链**：K≥3 打破 binary toward-null guarantee → 5/7 拓扑有 sign-flip guarantee（3 formal proof + 7 MC）→ exposure 83.4% + IV 52.6% sign reversal → ASV 三类型量化脆弱性谱 → FWL invariance 证明 4/7 拓扑矩阵校正无效 → PPI++ 93.8% 但需 gold labels
- **最容易判断错的事**：(1) bias_pct 在 JSON 中存储为**分数**（如 0.421 = 42.1%），不是已乘 100 的百分比——gen_diagnostic_plots.py 的 `*100` 是正确的；(2) §4.6 的 "63 scenarios" 包含 failed config 的 7 个 trivially-safe 条目，不是 56；(3) exposure unsafe 是 7/9=77.8% 不是 8/9，其余三个 unsafe 拓扑（mediation/frontdoor/iv）才是 8/9=88.9%
- **容易踩的坑**：不要把 Qwen2.5-7B 本地结果与 HumAID frontier 结果等同对待——模型档次差一个数量级；VAST 46% 精度的混淆矩阵不能作为 "practical LLM annotation" 的代表

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate the multi-class bias taxonomy and ASV framework through analytical derivations validated by Monte Carlo simulation, covering seven canonical DAG topologies under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification.
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	We study seven DAG topologies where annotation variable $A$ enters at structurally distinct positions: confounding, exposure, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door, and instrumental variable (IV). For each topology, we sample $K{\times}K$ confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior (each column independently), generating 100{,}000 random matrices per configuration. Each matrix defines a non-differential misclassification mechanism $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k)$.
10	
11	For every sampled $C$, we compute the analytical probability limit of the causal estimator and validate it against Monte Carlo simulation with $N{=}10{,}000$ samples per replication. ASV experiments use the parameterization $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$, where $\delta{=}0$ recovers perfect classification and $\delta{=}1$ yields the reference matrix $C_0$.
12	
13	\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}
14	\label{sec:taxonomy_results}
15	
16	\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}
17	Analytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates within 95\% confidence intervals across all seven topologies and all sampled confusion matrices, confirming the closed-form bias expressions derived in Section~\ref{sec:method}.
18	
19	\paragraph{Exposure DAG: catastrophic sign reversal.}
20	Under the full Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, 85\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices produce bias that violates the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee. More critically, 83.4\% of matrices cause \emph{sign reversal} of at least one coefficient. The two failure modes are distinct: for a coefficient with true value $\beta_1{=}1.0$, a near-permutation $C$ produces $\hat{\gamma}_1{=}{-}0.84$ (sign reversal, magnitude $0.84{\times}$); for $\beta_2{=}{-}0.5$, the same $C$ yields $\hat{\gamma}_2{=}{-}1.41$ (same-sign amplification, $2.83{\times}$).
21	
22	This finding persists in practically relevant subspaces. When restricting to confusion matrices with diagonal entries $\geq 0.7$ (typical of current LLM classifiers achieving $F_1 > 0.8$), the violation rate remains 13.7\% with 2.1\% sign reversal and 11.7\% amplification. Even at diagonal $\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\%. In contrast, the $K{=}2$ control confirms that all six tested binary confusion matrices maintain the classical bias-toward-null guarantee~\cite{brenner1993}, establishing that the multi-class failure is structurally distinct from the binary case.
23	
24	\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}
25	Five of seven topologies exhibit a structural guarantee: $\tau$-specific sign flip rate equals exactly zero across all sampled confusion matrices (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}). Confounding, mediation, and front-door topologies show 100\% violation and amplification rates but never reverse the sign of the target causal parameter. Collider misclassification attenuates the spurious association induced by conditioning, providing a form of protection. M-bias shows the weakest sensitivity overall (53.2\% violation, 10.4\% amplification) because annotation error on a collider variable reduces, rather than amplifies, the spurious M-bias path.
26	
27	\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}
28	The instrumental variable topology is the sole exception among non-exposure DAGs. The Wald estimator's first stage involves $C$ directly in the denominator, and 52.6\% of random confusion matrices cause the denominator to change sign, producing $\tau$-specific sign reversal. This rate remains stable across $K{=}3,5,7$, indicating that IV fragility is a structural property of the Wald estimator rather than a finite-$K$ artifact.
29	
30	\paragraph{Cross-$K$ robustness.}
31	Experiments at $K{=}5$ and $K{=}7$ (100{,}000 random matrices per topology) confirm that all structural patterns from $K{=}3$ persist. The sign-flip guarantee holds for the same five topologies, and IV sign-flip rates remain within 51--53\%. Worst-case bias magnitudes decrease modestly with $K$ for most topologies but increase for IV at low accuracy (worst case 14.0$\times$ at $K{=}3$ diagonal $\geq 0.5$).
32	
33	\begin{table}[t]
34	\centering
35	\caption{Bias characteristics of $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification across 7 DAG topologies. Violation: $|\hat{\tau}| > |\tau|$; Sign flip: $\text{sign}(\hat{\tau}) \neq \text{sign}(\tau)$; Amplification: $|\hat{\tau}| > |\tau|$ with same sign. Rates computed over 100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ random confusion matrices with $N{=}10{,}000$.}
36	\label{tab:taxonomy}
37	\small
38	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
39	\toprule
40	\textbf{Topology} & \textbf{Violation} & \textbf{Sign Flip} & \textbf{Amplif.} & $\tau_{\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\
41	\midrule
42	Confounding & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & \checkmark \\
43	Exposure & 85\% & 83.4\% & 14.1\% & \ding{55} \\
44	Mediation & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & \checkmark \\
45	Front-door & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & \checkmark \\
46	Collider & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & \checkmark \\
47	M-bias & 53.2\% & 0\% & 10.4\% & \checkmark \\
48	IV & 100\% & 52.6\% & 100\% & \ding{55} \\
49	\bottomrule
50	\end{tabular}
51	\end{table}
52	
53	The bias distributions across topologies are visualized in Figure~\ref{fig:bias_distribution}, where the exposure panel's dominant red region (sign reversal) contrasts with the symmetric attenuation patterns of other topologies.
54	
55	\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}
56	\label{sec:asv_results}
57	
58	\paragraph{Envelope monotonicity.}
59	The ASV envelope (bias magnitude as a function of $\delta$) is monotonically non-decreasing for all seven topologies, with zero violations across all tested scenarios. This validates the theoretical monotonicity property and confirms that $\delta$ serves as a meaningful ``dial'' controlling annotation error severity.
60	
61	\paragraph{Magnitude ASV.}
62	Table~\ref{tab:asv_grid} reports magnitude ASV ranges at $\tau{=}0.5$ with a 10\% bias threshold. The ranking from most fragile to most robust is: front-door (0.064--0.387) $<$ mediation (0.076--0.454) $<$ confounding (0.081--0.487) $<$ IV (0.101--0.607) $<$ collider (0.338--2.028) $<$ M-bias ($\infty$). The collider's high ASV reflects its protective nature: misclassification attenuates the spurious association rather than amplifying it. M-bias achieves infinite ASV because annotation error on the collider variable $A$ only reduces the already-small bias from the M-shaped confounding path.
63	
64	\paragraph{Significance power.}
65	At $\tau{=}0.05$, the significance ASV filter achieves 100\% power for confounding, front-door, and M-bias (96--100\%). Power drops to 0\% for mediation, collider, and IV. For mediation and collider, this is correct behavior: these are attenuating topologies where the biased effect at small $\tau$ falls below the detection threshold $z_{\alpha/2} \cdot SE$, and the ASV filter correctly blocks all rejections. For IV, the Wald estimator is immediately fragile ($\text{signif\_ASV} \approx 0$), reflecting its extreme sensitivity to any annotation error. Type~I error is 0\% across all topologies.
66	
67	\paragraph{Sign-flip ASV.}
68	For exposure, 83.4\% of reference matrices $C_0$ yield $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} < 1$, indicating catastrophic sensitivity to annotation error. For IV, sign-flip ASV ranges from 1.1 to 6.7, indicating moderate-to-high sensitivity depending on the reference matrix structure. All other topologies have undefined sign-flip ASV (no sign reversal is possible).
69	
70	The complete sensitivity ranking across ASV types is shown in Figure~\ref{fig:asv_ranking}, which reveals that different ASV types can yield different vulnerability orderings across topologies.
71	
72	\begin{table}[t]
73	\centering
74	\caption{ASV type applicability and sensitivity across DAG topologies. Magnitude ASV at $\tau{=}0.5$, 10\% threshold. Power at $\tau{=}0.05$ significance filter. \checkmark: effective and recommended; ($\checkmark$): applicable but less informative; ---: not applicable.}
75	\label{tab:asv_grid}
76	\small
77	\begin{tabular}{lcccl}
78	\toprule
79	\textbf{Topology} & \textbf{Mag. ASV} & \textbf{Signif.} & \textbf{Sign-flip} & \textbf{Rank} \\
80	\midrule
81	IV & 0.101--0.607 & 0\% & 1.1--6.7 & 1 (fragile) \\
82	Front-door & 0.064--0.387 & 100\% & --- & 2 \\
83	Confounding & 0.081--0.487 & 100\% & --- & 3 \\
84	Mediation & 0.076--0.454 & 0\% & --- & 4 \\
85	Collider & 0.338--2.028 & 0\% & --- & 5 \\
86	M-bias & $\infty$ & 96--100\% & --- & 6 (immune) \\
87	Exposure & --- & --- & 83.4\%$^*$ & --- \\
88	\bottomrule
89	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\scriptsize $^*$Fraction of $C_0$ with $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} < 1$. Exposure uses sign-flip ASV exclusively.}
90	\end{tabular}
91	\end{table}
92	
93	\subsection{$N$-Sensitivity}
94	\label{sec:n_sensitivity}
95	
96	The significance ASV exhibits a sharp phase transition: for mediation at $\tau{=}0.05$, ASV-filtered power is exactly 0 for $N{\leq}5{,}000$ (blocking all rejections), then jumps to 1.0 at $N{=}10{,}000$. The critical sample size $N^* \approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$ is analytically computable, providing practitioners with a concrete minimum sample size for their topology and effect size.
97	
98	\subsection{Binarization Collapse}
99	\label{sec:binarization}
100	
101	Collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\times$ across topologies: from $\bar{\Delta}{=}0.006$ (M-bias) to $\bar{\Delta}{=}0.926$ (IV). Exposure and IV also introduce sign flips during binarization (14\% and 17.5\%), a phenomenon absent in the other topologies. Full results are in Appendix~\ref{app:supplementary} (Figure~\ref{fig:binarization}).
102	
103	\subsection{Empirical Validation}
104	\label{sec:empirical}
105	
106	We validate the theoretical framework using real LLM confusion matrices estimated from HumAID~\cite{alam2021humaid}, a 10-class disaster response classification task ($K{=}10$). Three frontier LLMs (Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat) annotate HumAID samples under zero-shot and few-shot prompting, yielding 8 usable model$\times$prompt configurations (one configuration excluded due to API failure). Each configuration produces a $10{\times}10$ confusion matrix $C$, which we plug into the analytical bias expressions from Section~\ref{sec:method} for all seven topologies.
107	
108	\paragraph{Theory--practice alignment.}
109	The empirical bias ranking across topologies matches the theoretical predictions from the random-matrix analysis. IV produces the largest bias (12.4--42.1\% relative bias across configurations), followed by exposure (9.0--17.4\%), mediation and front-door (10.7--14.8\% each), while collider (4.1--5.6\%), confounding (4.9--6.2\%), and M-bias (5.1--6.5\%) remain below 10\%. This ordering is consistent with the ASV vulnerability spectrum from Section~\ref{sec:asv_results}: IV is maximally fragile and collider is near-immune. Zero sign reversals occur across all 56 topology$\times$configuration combinations ($8 \times 7$), consistent with the diagonal dominance of all estimated confusion matrices (mean diagonal 0.671--0.724).
110	
111	\paragraph{ASV diagnostic results.}
112	Of 63 scenario-level ASV evaluations ($9 \times 7$; the failed configuration contributes 7 trivially-safe entries), 49.2\% (31/63) are flagged as ASV-unsafe, indicating that roughly half of LLM annotation scenarios require correction or sensitivity reporting. Magnitude ASV values are triggered for four topologies: IV (0.31--0.83), mediation (0.62--0.90), front-door (0.62--0.91), and exposure (0.65--1.11). The remaining three topologies (confounding, collider, M-bias) produce bias below the 10\% threshold across all configurations, requiring no ASV report (Figure~\ref{fig:asv_heatmap}). Sign-flip ASV is undefined for all configurations, consistent with the zero observed sign reversals at $K{=}10$ with diagonal-dominant $C$.
113	
114	\paragraph{Confusion matrix structure.}
115	Macro $F_1$ ranges from 0.614 to 0.690 across models, but $F_1$ does not predict downstream causal bias: within this narrow range, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude---from 4.1\% (collider) to 42.1\% (IV)---demonstrating that topology, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk (Figure~\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}). Inter-model error correlations are low (0.051--0.157), favorable for multi-annotator correction with model-diverse gold-label subsets.
116	
117	\begin{figure}[t]
118	\centering
119	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}
120	\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not F1. Each point represents one LLM annotator $\times$ topology combination from HumAID ($K{=}10$, 8 configurations $\times$ 7 topologies $=$ 56 points). Within a narrow $F_1$ range (0.614--0.690), relative bias varies $10{\times}$: IV reaches 42.1\% while collider stays below 5.6\%.}
121	\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}
122	\end{figure}
123	
124	
125	\subsection{Correction Guidance}
126	\label{sec:correction}
127	
128	We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies under six error structures (210 configurations total): naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX, and prediction-powered inference (PPI++)~\cite{angelopoulos2023}. Full per-topology results are in Table~\ref{tab:correction} and the decision procedure in Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix~\ref{app:supplementary}).
129	
130	\paragraph{FWL invariance.}
131	For four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias), the Frisch--Waugh--Lovell theorem implies that applying $C^{-1}$ to the dummy variables cannot change the $T$ coefficient. Naive plug-in and MLA achieve exactly 0.0\% bias reduction across these four topologies.
132	
133	\paragraph{Method ranking.}
134	PPI++ achieves the highest grand-mean bias reduction (93.8\%) with nominal coverage (96.8\%), but requires gold-standard labels. MC-SIMEX provides moderate gold-free correction (20.9\%), serving as the only viable fallback for IV (64.7\% reduction). DSL corrects confounding (49.8\%) and mediation (70.3\%) but catastrophically amplifies bias for M-bias ($-$768\%). No single method dominates: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent.
135

Rates computed over 100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ random confusion matrices with $N{=}10{,}000$.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & \\ding{55} \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & \\ding{55} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions across topologies are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}, where the exposure panel's dominant red region (sign reversal) contrasts with the symmetric attenuation patterns of other topologies.\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Envelope monotonicity.}\nThe ASV envelope (bias magnitude as a function of $\\delta$) is monotonically non-decreasing for all seven topologies, with zero violations across all tested scenarios. This validates the theoretical monotonicity property and confirms that $\\delta$ serves as a meaningful ``dial'' controlling annotation error severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Magnitude ASV.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:asv_grid} reports magnitude ASV ranges at $\\tau{=}0.5$ with a 10\\% bias threshold. The ranking from most fragile to most robust is: front-door (0.064--0.387) $<$ mediation (0.076--0.454) $<$ confounding (0.081--0.487) $<$ IV (0.101--0.607) $<$ collider (0.338--2.028) $<$ M-bias ($\\infty$). The collider's high ASV reflects its protective nature: misclassification attenuates the spurious association rather than amplifying it. M-bias achieves infinite ASV because annotation error on the collider variable $A$ only reduces the already-small bias from the M-shaped confounding path.\n\n\\paragraph{Significance power.}\nAt $\\tau{=}0.05$, the significance ASV filter achieves 100\\% power for confounding, front-door, and M-bias (96--100\\%). For mediation and collider, this is correct behavior: these are attenuating topologies where the biased effect at small $\\tau$ falls below the detection threshold $z_{\\alpha/2} \\cdot SE$, and the ASV filter correctly blocks all rejections. For IV, the Wald estimator is immediately fragile ($\\text{signif\\_ASV} \\approx 0$), reflecting its extreme sensitivity to any annotation error. Type~I error is 0\\% across all topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Sign-flip ASV.}\nFor exposure, 83.4\\% of reference matrices $C_0$ yield $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} < 1$, indicating catastrophic sensitivity to annotation error. ASV} & \\textbf{Signif.} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} & \\textbf{Rank} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV & 0.101--0.607 & 0\\% & 1.1--6.7 & 1 (fragile) \\\\\nFront-door & 0.064--0.387 & 100\\% & --- & 2 \\\\\nConfounding & 0.081--0.487 & 100\\% & --- & 3 \\\\\nMediation & 0.076--0.454 & 0\\% & --- & 4 \\\\\nCollider & 0.338--2.028 & 0\\% & --- & 5 \\\\\nM-bias & $\\infty$ & 96--100\\% & --- & 6 (immune) \\\\\nExposure & --- & --- & 83.4\\%$^*$ & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\scriptsize $^*$Fraction of $C_0$ with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} < 1$. Exposure uses sign-flip ASV exclusively.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{$N$-Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:n_sensitivity}\n\nThe significance ASV exhibits a sharp phase transition: for mediation at $\\tau{=}0.05$, ASV-filtered power is exactly 0 for $N{\\leq}5{,}000$ (blocking all rejections), then jumps to 1.0 at $N{=}10{,}000$. Full results are in Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary} (Figure~\\ref{fig:binarization}).\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate the theoretical framework using real LLM confusion matrices estimated from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid}, a 10-class disaster response classification task ($K{=}10$). Three frontier LLMs (Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat) annotate HumAID samples under zero-shot and few-shot prompting, yielding 8 usable model$\\times$prompt configurations (one configuration excluded due to API failure). Each configuration produces a $10{\\times}10$ confusion matrix $C$, which we plug into the analytical bias expressions from Section~\\ref{sec:method} for all seven topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Theory--practice alignment.}\nThe empirical bias ranking across topologies matches the theoretical predictions from the random-matrix analysis. IV produces the largest bias (12.4--42.1\\% relative bias across configurations), followed by exposure (9.0--17.4\\%), mediation and front-door (10.7--14.8\\% each), while collider (4.1--5.6\\%), confounding (4.9--6.2\\%), and M-bias (5.1--6.5\\%) remain below 10\\%. This ordering is consistent with the ASV vulnerability spectrum from Section~\\ref{sec:asv_results}: IV is maximally fragile and collider is near-immune. Zero sign reversals occur across all 56 topology$\\times$configuration combinations ($8 \\times 7$), consistent with the diagonal dominance of all estimated confusion matrices (mean diagonal 0.671--0.724).\n\n\\paragraph{ASV diagnostic results.}\nOf 63 scenario-level ASV evaluations ($9 \\times 7$; the failed configuration contributes 7 trivially-safe entries), 49.2\\% (31/63) are flagged as ASV-unsafe, indicating that roughly half of LLM annotation scenarios require correction or sensitivity reporting. The remaining three topologies (confounding, collider, M-bias) produce bias below the 10\\% threshold across all configurations, requiring no ASV report (Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_heatmap}). Inter-model error correlations are low (0.051--0.157), favorable for multi-annotator correction with model-diverse gold-label subsets.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not F1. Each point represents one LLM annotator $\\times$ topology combination from HumAID ($K{=}10$, 8 configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies $=$ 56 points). Within a narrow $F_1$ range (0.614--0.690), relative bias varies $10{\\times}$: IV reaches 42.1\\% while collider stays below 5.6\\%.}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies under six error structures (210 configurations total): naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX, and prediction-powered inference (PPI++)~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}. Full per-topology results are in Table~\\ref{tab:correction} and the decision procedure in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}).\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nFor four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias), the Frisch--Waugh--Lovell theorem implies that applying $C^{-1}$ to the dummy variables cannot change the $T$ coefficient. Naive plug-in and MLA achieve exactly 0.0\\% bias reduction across these four topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest grand-mean bias reduction (93.8\\%) with nominal coverage (96.8\\%), but requires gold-standard labels. MC-SIMEX provides moderate gold-free correction (20.9\\%), serving as the only viable fallback for IV (64.7\\% reduction). DSL corrects confounding (49.8\\%) and mediation (70.3\\%) but catastrophically amplifies bias for M-bias ($-$768\\%). --- a/docs/paper/abstract.tex

@@ -1,3 +1,3 @@
 \begin{abstract}
-Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as annotators in computational social science, yet annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that binary measurement error theory cannot predict. We present the first systematic framework characterizing how $K$-class confusion matrix structure interacts with DAG topology to determine bias direction and magnitude. We derive closed-form bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies under multi-class non-differential misclassification and prove a structural sign-flip guarantee: five topologies (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door) never produce sign reversal regardless of the confusion matrix, while exposure and instrumental variable topologies can. In the exposure DAG, 83.4\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices cause sign reversal, a failure that persists at 13.7\% violation rate even with classifier accuracy above 70\%. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), a sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures that reveal a topology-dependent vulnerability spectrum: IV estimates are maximally fragile (ASV $= 0.101$--$0.607$) while M-bias adjustment is immune (ASV $= \infty$). We evaluate five correction methods across all topologies and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve 0\% bias reduction on the target coefficient for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\% reduction but requires gold labels. Binarization from multi-class to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\times$ across topologies. Neither classification $F_1$ nor correction method choice alone determines downstream safety; topology-aware analysis is necessary for valid causal inference with LLM-generated annotations.
+Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as annotators in computational social science, yet annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that binary measurement error theory cannot predict. We present the first systematic framework characterizing how $K$-class confusion matrix structure interacts with DAG topology to determine bias direction and magnitude. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies under multi-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven) and establish a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven): five topologies (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door) never produce sign reversal regardless of the confusion matrix, while exposure and instrumental variable topologies can. In the exposure DAG, 83.4\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices cause sign reversal, a failure that persists at 13.7\% violation rate (2.1\% sign reversal, 11.7\% amplification) even with classifier accuracy above 70\%. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), a sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures that reveal a topology-dependent vulnerability spectrum: IV estimates are maximally vulnerable (52.6\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) while M-bias adjustment is immune (ASV $= \infty$). We evaluate five correction methods across all topologies and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve 0\% bias reduction on the target coefficient for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\% reduction but requires gold labels. Binarization from multi-class to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\times$ across topologies. Neither classification $F_1$ nor correction method choice alone determines downstream safety; topology-aware analysis is necessary for valid causal inference with LLM-generated annotations.
 \end{abstract}

@@ -3,7 +3,7 @@
 
 Large language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\% for smaller models. Even classifiers achieving $F_1 > 0.93$ produce over 50\% downstream error risk.
 
-The consequences can be catastrophic. Under $K{=}3$ non-differential exposure misclassification, 83.4\% of confusion matrices cause \emph{sign reversal} of the estimated treatment effect, where the estimated direction of the causal relationship is opposite to the truth. This failure breaks the classical binary guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. The breakdown is not an edge case: it persists even when classifier accuracy exceeds 70\% (13.7\% violation rate at diagonal $\geq 0.7$).
+The consequences can be catastrophic. Under $K{=}3$ non-differential exposure misclassification, 83.4\% of confusion matrices cause \emph{sign reversal} of the estimated treatment effect, where the estimated direction of the causal relationship is opposite to the truth. This failure breaks the classical binary guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. The breakdown is not an edge case: it persists even when classifier accuracy exceeds 70\% (13.7\% violation rate at diagonal $\geq 0.7$, comprising 2.1\% sign reversal and 11.7\% amplification).
 
 Why does the multi-class setting behave so differently? For binary classification, non-differential error can only attenuate the effect because the $2{\times}2$ stochastic confusion matrix preserves coefficient signs. With $K \geq 3$ categories, the confusion matrix can assign negative effective weights to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal~\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This fundamental asymmetry between binary and multi-class annotation error has received limited systematic treatment. \citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). No existing framework addresses how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across different causal identification strategies.

 We address these gaps with three contributions:

This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone.

We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven) ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. ```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Large language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\% for smaller models; these errors are highly correlated across models (60\% agreement when both err)~\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.
5	
6	The consequences can be catastrophic. Under $K{=}3$ non-differential exposure misclassification, 83.4\% of confusion matrices cause \emph{sign reversal} of the estimated treatment effect, where the estimated direction of the causal relationship is opposite to the truth. This failure breaks the classical binary guarantee that non-differential misclassification biases estimates ``toward the null''~\cite{brenner1993}, a guarantee that many applied researchers implicitly rely upon. The breakdown is not an edge case: it persists even when classifier accuracy exceeds 70\% (13.7\% violation rate at diagonal $\geq 0.7$). Moreover, standard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies (from 4.1\% to 42.1\%), illustrating that \emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk.
7	
8	The mechanism is algebraic. For binary classification, non-differential error can only attenuate the effect because the $2{\times}2$ stochastic confusion matrix preserves coefficient signs. With $K \geq 3$ categories, the confusion matrix can assign negative effective weights to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal~\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). No existing framework addresses how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across different causal identification strategies.
9	
10	We address these gaps with three contributions:
11	
12	\begin{enumerate}
13	\item \textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven). We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone. The taxonomy extends to binarization: collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary introduces additional bias varying up to 147$\times$ across topologies.
14	
15	\item \textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.
16	
17	\item \textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies and find a negative result with significant practical implications that, while theoretically predicted by Frisch--Waugh--Lovell, has been overlooked in the annotation correction literature: matrix-based corrections (naive plug-in, MLA) achieve exactly 0\% bias reduction for four of seven topologies, because the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible confusion-matrix transformations. Among gold-free methods, only MC-SIMEX provides positive bias reduction (20.9\% grand mean). PPI++ achieves 93.8\% bias reduction with nominal coverage but requires gold-standard labels.
18	\end{enumerate}
19	
```

This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \\citet{brenner1993} identified the possibility of polytomous reversal but did not characterize it across causal designs. \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology (unobserved confounding). No existing framework addresses how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across different causal identification strategies.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification (closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven). We establish that five topologies maintain a structural sign-flip guarantee (with formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven), while exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal. This taxonomy reveals that bias direction is determined by the \\emph{interaction} between the confusion matrix and the DAG topology, not by either alone. The taxonomy extends to binarization: collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary introduces additional bias varying up to 147$\\times$ across topologies.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs).} We propose a unified sensitivity analysis framework with three complementary measures: magnitude ASV (E-value analogue for annotation error), significance ASV (hypothesis test filter for attenuating topologies), and sign-flip ASV (for exposure and IV). ASVs reveal a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\\% sign reversal; sign-flip ASV $= 1.1$--$6.7$) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$), providing practitioners with topology-aware robustness diagnostics.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies and find a negative result with significant practical implications that, while theoretically predicted by Frisch--Waugh--Lovell, has been overlooked in the annotation correction literature: matrix-based corrections (naive plug-in, MLA) achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction for four of seven topologies, because the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible confusion-matrix transformations. Among gold-free methods, only MC-SIMEX provides positive bias reduction (20.9\\% grand mean). PPI++ achieves 93.8\\% bias reduction with nominal coverage but requires gold-standard labels.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    }

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We presented the first systematic characterization of how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology. Our bias taxonomy across seven topologies reveals that the classical binary guarantee of bias toward the null breaks for $K \geq 3$: exposure and IV topologies can produce sign reversal (83.4\% and 52.6\% respectively), while five topologies maintain a structural guarantee of no sign flip. The ASV framework provides topology-aware sensitivity diagnostics, revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune). Our correction guidance analysis reveals that correction method effectiveness is itself topology-dependent: a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result proves matrix-based corrections are ineffective for four topologies, while PPI++ achieves 93.8\% bias reduction at the cost of gold-standard labels. Binarization collapse analysis further demonstrates that simplifying multi-class annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 147$\times$ across topologies.
5	
6	\paragraph{Limitations.}
7	Our framework assumes non-differential misclassification: $P(A^*|A, Y, \mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*|A)$. This holds when the LLM annotator has no access to the outcome $Y$, but may be violated when few-shot examples are selected conditional on $Y$ or when annotation difficulty varies with the outcome. Under differential error, the bias expressions no longer hold and the ASV framework provides an upper bound rather than an exact characterization. Closed-form bias expressions are derived for $K{=}3$ and verified numerically at $K{=}5,7$; expressions for arbitrary $K$ remain open. The framework assumes a single annotator; extending to correlated multi-annotator settings~\cite{kim_garg2025} is an open problem. The empirical validation uses real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID ($K{=}10$, three frontier models); validation at $K{=}3$ and $K{=}2$ is ongoing.
8	
9	\paragraph{Future work.}
10	Immediate directions include extending to differential misclassification, incorporating correlated multi-LLM error structures into correction strategies, and developing a practitioner toolkit that computes ASVs from estimated confusion matrices and routes to topology-appropriate correction methods.
11	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}
5	The epidemiology literature has long studied misclassification bias through causal diagrams~\cite{gustafson2004}. \citet{hernan_cole2009} formalized the starred-node DAG framework for reasoning about measurement bias, distinguishing differential from non-differential error. For binary variables, \citet{ogburn_vanderweele2012} proved that non-differential confounder misclassification produces adjusted estimates between the crude and true values, precluding sign reversal. \citet{brenner1993} showed this guarantee breaks for polytomous confounders, and \citet{veirod_laake2001} derived bias expressions for multi-category exposure misclassification in Poisson regression. \citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} further demonstrated that dependent misclassification of covariate and exposure can bias in either direction. However, none of these works systematically characterize how bias varies \emph{across} DAG topologies for a given confusion matrix. Our taxonomy fills this gap: given the same $K$-class confusion matrix, we derive how bias direction and magnitude differ across seven canonical topologies.
6	
7	\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation in causal studies.}
8	\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to text classifier measurement error. \citet{teblunthuis2024} provided the most complete binary treatment: four DAG scenarios with testable conditions and a Maximum Likelihood Adjustment method, but restricted to binary classification. \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only unobserved confounding. On the annotation quality side, \citet{baumann2025} demonstrated that 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with LLM annotations, \citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\% error correlation across models, and \citet{messing2026} decomposed LLM pipeline variance but explicitly excluded causal identification. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose annotation quality without connecting it to causal estimate validity. Our framework bridges this gap: we provide the causal-analytic link from multi-class confusion matrix structure to topology-specific bias, sensitivity, and correction.
9	
10	\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis for observational studies.}
11	Our ASV framework draws on the tradition of sensitivity analysis for unmeasured confounding~\cite{rosenbaum2002}. \citet{vanderweele_ding2017} introduced E-values as interpretable sensitivity measures: the minimum strength of unmeasured confounding needed to explain away an observed association. \citet{ding_vanderweele2016} developed assumption-free sensitivity bounds, and \citet{smith_vanderweele2021} proposed bounds for multiple bias sources including misclassification. Our ASVs adapt the E-value philosophy to a different bias source: rather than quantifying unmeasured confounding strength, ASVs quantify the annotation error amplification $\delta$ needed to reach a dangerous bias threshold. Unlike the scalar E-value, ASVs comprise three complementary types (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip), because annotation error failure modes are topology-dependent.
12	
13	\paragraph{Correction methods.}
14	Prediction-powered inference~\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, gligoric2025} provide general frameworks for valid inference with ML-generated labels, combining predicted labels with small gold-standard samples. \citet{zrnic_candes2024} extended PPI via cross-prediction for improved efficiency. \citet{audinet2025} benchmarked these methods across datasets. These correction methods are designed as general-purpose tools, agnostic to DAG topology. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four of seven topologies. Our ASV analysis identifies \emph{when} correction is needed and \emph{which} failure mode to address, while PPI/DSL provide the \emph{how}.
15	
```

      "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}\nThe epidemiology literature has long studied misclassification bias through causal diagrams~\\cite{gustafson2004}. \\citet{hernan_cole2009} formalized the starred-node DAG framework for reasoning about measurement bias, distinguishing differential from non-differential error. For binary variables, \\citet{ogburn_vanderweele2012} proved that non-differential confounder misclassification produces adjusted estimates between the crude and true values, precluding sign reversal. \\citet{brenner1993} showed this guarantee breaks for polytomous confounders, and \\citet{veirod_laake2001} derived bias expressions for multi-category exposure misclassification in Poisson regression. \\citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} further demonstrated that dependent misclassification of covariate and exposure can bias in either direction. However, none of these works systematically characterize how bias varies \\emph{across} DAG topologies for a given confusion matrix. These correction methods are designed as general-purpose tools, agnostic to DAG topology.
