 \subsection{Empirical Validation}
 \label{sec:empirical}
 
-\textit{Empirical validation using confusion matrices from three LLMs (Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat) on three CSS classification tasks (HumAID, VAST, CivilComments) is in progress. Results will be incorporated upon completion.}
-
-\subsection{Correction Guidance}
-\label{sec:correction}
+We validate the theoretical framework using real LLM confusion matrices estimated from HumAID~\cite{alam2021humaid}, a 10-class disaster response classification task ($K{=}10$). Three frontier LLMs (Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat) annotate HumAID samples under zero-shot and few-shot prompting, yielding 8 usable model$\times$prompt configurations (one configuration excluded due to API failure). Each configuration produces a $10{\times}10$ confusion matrix $C$, which we plug into the analytical bias expressions from Section~\ref{sec:method} for all seven topologies.
 
-We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies under six error structures (210 configurations total): naive plug-in (replace $A$ with $A^*$ after applying $C^{-1}$), maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX, and prediction-powered inference (PPI++)~\cite{angelopoulos2023}.
+\paragraph{Theory--practice alignment.}
+The empirical bias ranking across topologies matches the theoretical predictions from the random-matrix analysis. IV produces the largest bias (12.4--42.1\% relative bias across configurations), followed by exposure (9.0--17.4\%), mediation and front-door (10.7--14.8\% each), while collider (4.1--5.6\%), confounding (4.9--6.2\%), and M-bias (5.1--6.5\%) remain below 10\%. This ordering is consistent with the ASV vulnerability spectrum from Section~\ref{sec:asv_results}: IV is maximally fragile and collider is near-immune. Zero sign reversals occur across all 56 topology$\times$configuration combinations ($8 \times 7$), consistent with the diagonal dominance of all estimated confusion matrices (mean diagonal 0.671--0.724).
 
-\paragraph{Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance.}
-For four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate rather than the estimand (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias), the Frisch--Waugh--Lovell theorem implies that applying $C^{-1}$ to the dummy variables cannot change the coefficient on $T$: the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible transformations of the regressors. Naive plug-in and MLA achieve exactly 0.0\% bias reduction on the $T$ coefficient across all four topologies, confirming this theoretical prediction.
+\paragraph{ASV diagnostic results.}
+Of 63 scenario-level ASV evaluations ($9 \times 7$; the failed configuration contributes 7 trivially-safe entries), 49.2\% (31/63) are flagged as ASV-unsafe, indicating that roughly half of LLM annotation scenarios require correction or sensitivity reporting. Magnitude ASV values are triggered for four topologies: IV (0.31--0.83), mediation (0.62--0.90), front-door (0.62--0.91), and exposure (0.65--1.11). The remaining three topologies (confounding, collider, M-bias) produce bias below the 10\% threshold across all configurations, requiring no ASV report (Figure~\ref{fig:asv_heatmap}). Sign-flip ASV is undefined for all configurations, consistent with the zero observed sign reversals at $K{=}10$ with diagonal-dominant $C$.
 
-\paragraph{Method ranking.}
-PPI++ achieves the highest grand-mean bias reduction (93.8\%) and is the only method to reach nominal coverage (96.8\%). MC-SIMEX provides moderate correction (20.9\% bias reduction) without requiring gold-standard labels but achieves only 37.6\% coverage. DSL, naive plug-in, and MLA produce negative grand-mean bias reduction ($-$144.8\%, $-$20.0\%, $-$37.4\% respectively), indicating that on average these methods increase bias across topologies.
+\paragraph{Confusion matrix structure.}
+Macro $F_1$ ranges from 0.614 to 0.690 across models, but $F_1$ does not predict downstream causal bias: within this narrow range, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude---from 4.1\% (collider) to 42.1\% (IV)---demonstrating that topology, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk (Figure~\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}). Inter-model error correlations are low (0.051--0.157), favorable for multi-annotator correction with model-diverse gold-label subsets.

Results will be incorporated upon completion.}\n-\n-\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n-\\label{sec:correction}\n+We validate the theoretical framework using real LLM confusion matrices estimated from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid}, a 10-class disaster response classification task ($K{=}10$). Three frontier LLMs (Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat) annotate HumAID samples under zero-shot and few-shot prompting, yielding 8 usable model$\\times$prompt configurations (one configuration excluded due to API failure). Each configuration produces a $10{\\times}10$ confusion matrix $C$, which we plug into the analytical bias expressions from Section~\\ref{sec:method} for all seven topologies.\n \n-We evaluate five correction methods across all seven topologies under six error structures (210 configurations total): naive plug-in (replace $A$ with $A^*$ after applying $C^{-1}$), maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX, and prediction-powered inference (PPI++)~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}.\n+\\paragraph{Theory--practice alignment.}\n+The empirical bias ranking across topologies matches the theoretical predictions from the random-matrix analysis. IV produces the largest bias (12.4--42.1\\% relative bias across configurations), followed by exposure (9.0--17.4\\%), mediation and front-door (10.7--14.8\\% each), while collider (4.1--5.6\\%), confounding (4.9--6.2\\%), and M-bias (5.1--6.5\\%) remain below 10\\%. This ordering is consistent with the ASV vulnerability spectrum from Section~\\ref{sec:asv_results}: IV is maximally fragile and collider is near-immune. Zero sign reversals occur across all 56 topology$\\times$configuration combinations ($8 \\times 7$), consistent with the diagonal dominance of all estimated confusion matrices (mean diagonal 0.671--0.724).\n \n-\\paragraph{Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance.}\n-For four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate rather than the estimand (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias), the Frisch--Waugh--Lovell theorem implies that applying $C^{-1}$ to the dummy variables cannot change the coefficient on $T$: the column space of the design matrix is invariant to invertible transformations of the regressors. Naive plug-in and MLA achieve exactly 0.0\\% bias reduction on the $T$ coefficient across all four topologies, confirming this theoretical prediction.\n+\\paragraph{ASV diagnostic results.}\n+Of 63 scenario-level ASV evaluations ($9 \\times 7$; the failed configuration contributes 7 trivially-safe entries), 49.2\\% (31/63) are flagged as ASV-unsafe, indicating that roughly half of LLM annotation scenarios require correction or sensitivity reporting. Magnitude ASV values are triggered for four topologies: IV (0.31--0.83), mediation (0.62--0.90), front-door (0.62--0.91), and exposure (0.65--1.11). The remaining three topologies (confounding, collider, M-bias) produce bias below the 10\\% threshold across all configurations, requiring no ASV report (Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_heatmap}). Sign-flip ASV is undefined for all configurations, consistent with the zero observed sign reversals at $K{=}10$ with diagonal-dominant $C$.\n \n-\\paragraph{Method ranking.}\n-PPI++ achieves the highest grand-mean bias reduction (93.8\\%) and is the only method to reach nominal coverage (96.8\\%). MC-SIMEX provides moderate correction (20.9\\% bias reduction) without requiring gold-standard labels but achieves only 37.6\\% coverage. DSL, naive plug-in, and MLA produce negative grand-mean bias reduction ($-$144.8\\%, $-$20.0\\%, $-$37.4\\% respectively), indicating that on average these methods increase bias across topologies.\n+\\paragraph{Confusion matrix structure.}\n+Macro $F_1$ ranges from 0.614 to 0.690 across models, but $F_1$ does not predict downstream causal bias: within this narrow range, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude---from 4.1\\% (collider) to 42.1\\% (IV)---demonstrating that topology, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk (Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}). Inter-model error correlations are low (0.051--0.157), favorable for multi-annotator correction with model-diverse gold-label subsets.\n \n-\\begin{table}[t]\n+\\begin{figure}[t]\n \\centering\n-\\caption{Bias reduction (\\%) by correction method and DAG topology (mean across 6 error structures). Positive = reduced bias; negative = increased bias. Naive/MLA show 0.0\\% for FWL-invariant topologies because $C^{-1}$ cannot change the $T$ coefficient. Bold: best method per topology.}\n-\\label{tab:correction}\n-\\small\n-\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n-\\toprule\n-\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Naive} & \\textbf{MLA} & \\textbf{DSL} & \\textbf{SIMEX} & \\textbf{PPI++} \\\\\n-\\midrule\n-Confounding & 0.0 & 0.0 & 49.8 & 30.1 & \\textbf{97.8} \\\\\n-Mediation & 0.0 & 0.0 & \\textbf{70.3} & 24.6 & 99.3 \\\\\n-Collider & 0.0 & 0.0 & $-$89 & 12.4 & \\textbf{98.6} \\\\\n-M-bias & 0.0 & 0.0 & $-$768 & 5.1 & \\textbf{63.7} \\\\\n-\\midrule\n-Front-door & $<$0 & $<$0 & $-$29 & 8.7 & \\textbf{99.9} \\\\\n-Exposure & $<$0 & $<$0 & $-$60 & 1.0 & \\textbf{98.9} \\\\\n-IV & $<$0 & $<$0 & $-$187 & \\textbf{64.7} & 98.4 \\\\\n-\\midrule",

```text
Now I have the authoritative source data from the server. Let me compare:

**Server (method_comparison_summary.txt) — source of truth:**

| Topology | Naive | MLA | DSL | SIMEX | PPI++ |
|----------|-------|-----|-----|-------|-------|
| confounding | -0.0% | 0.0% | 49.8% | 35.9% | 99.3% |
| mediation | -0.0% | 0.0% | 70.3% | 42.4% | 98.8% |


| collider | 0.0% | -0.0% | -89.2% | 37.1% | 97.0% |
| exposure | -45.6% | -89.9% | -59.6% | -3.8% | 98.8% |
| mbias | 0.0% | 0.0% | -768.0% | 25.7% | 63.7% |
| iv | -40.9% | -59.2% | -187.4% | 64.7% | 99.5% |
| frontdoor | -53.5% | -112.5% | -29.2% | -55. |

Generating markdown table... The appendix table matches perfectly with the server data, which is a good sign. Now I'm checking the committed experiments table to see if there are any discrepancies in how the results were recorded. I'm noticing significant discrepancies between the numbers in the committed experiments.tex table and the actual values I'm seeing—SIMEX has substantial differences across nearly every causal scenario, and PPI++ also shows notable mismatches that need to be corrected. The grand means actually match between the two versions, which is suspicious—it suggests the Agent back-calculated fake per-topology values that would average to the correct total. This is particularly dangerous because it means a simple sanity check on the grand mean would pass, masking the fabrication. But looking more closely at the committed table, the individual SIMEX values don't align with what they should be, and both tables claim to represent the same underlying data. the committed experiments.tex table contains fabricated per-topology values for SIMEX and PPI++, while the working tree version correctly uses the actual numbers from the appendix. This is a serious issue—the Paper Agent likely generated the table from grand means without verifying the underlying per-topology data, and the working tree fix needs to be committed to correct this.

I'm verifying the Naive and MLA numbers across topologies. The server data shows negative signs for some values that should be zero due to floating point precision, but the appendix correctly displays them as 0.0 for the FWL-invariant cases. For the non-FWL topologies like front-door, exposure, and IV, both the server and appendix match. Now I'm checking that the grand means calculations align—the Naive grand mean of -20.0% and MLA grand mean of -37.4% both check out against the individual topology values.

Continuing through the remaining metrics: DSL grand mean comes to -144.8%, PPI grand mean is 93.8%, and SIMEX grand mean is 20.9%. All the appendix grand means are verified and consistent.

Now I'm compiling my full review. I've found a critical issue: the committed version has a Table 3 in experiments.tex with SIMEX and PPI++ values that appear to be fabricated—they don't match what the server source data actually produces. The SIMEX numbers in particular show a systematic discrepancy. I've moved binarization into Contribution 1 and cleaned up the em-dash styling, and there are several uncommitted improvements in the working tree—table consolidation, section condensing, expanded empirical section, and new proofs for collider and front-door cases—that all need to be committed. The AAAI style file still needs updating, Table 1's caption correction is done, and I've removed the internal reference from the IV paragraph. Now I'm ready to send this review to the Director.

{
  "text": "## 审查报告 — Step 5 Round 1 修复验证 + 论文全文交叉核实\n\n**结论**: CRITICAL（1 项 critical 需立即处理，2 项 warning 建议下轮修复）\n\n### 发现\n\n**[severity: critical] Committed f28da30 的 experiments.tex Table 3 包含错误的 per-topology SIMEX/PPI++ 数值**\n\n对比服务器源数据 (`artifacts/plan007/method_comparison_summary.txt`) 和 committed experiments.tex 中的 Table 3：\n\n| Topology | SIMEX(committed) | SIMEX(实际) | PPI++(committed) | PPI++(实际) |\n|----------|-----------------|------------|-----------------|------------|\n| Confounding | 30.1 | **35.9** | 97.8 | **99.3** |\n| Mediation | 24.6 | **42.4** | 99.3 | **98.8** |\n| Collider | 12.4 | **37.1** | 98.6 | **97.0** |\n| M-bias | 5.1 | **25.7** | 63.7 | 63.7 ✓ |\n| Front-door | 8.7 | **-55.7** | 99.9 | **99.3** |\n| Exposure | 1.0 | **-3.8** | 98.9 | **98.8** |\n| IV | 64.7 | 64.7 ✓ | 98.4 | **99.5** |\n\ncommitted 版的 SIMEX 系统性偏高（front-door 实际 -55.7% 被写成 +8.7%），使 SIMEX 看起来远优于实际表现。两组数据的 grand mean 恰好都是 20.9%——Paper Agent 可能是用 grand mean 反推了假的 per-topology 值。\n\n**当前状态**：working tree 已修复——experiments.tex 移除了内联表格，appendix 中的 Table 3 数值与服务器完全吻合。但这些修改**未 commit**，committed 版本仍有错误数据。**必须立即 commit working tree 变更。**\n\n---\n\n**[severity: warning] §4.7 Correction Guidance 文字对 MC-SIMEX 的描述有 cherry-picking 风险**\n\nexperiments.tex L133-134：\"MC-SIMEX provides moderate gold-free correction (20.9%), serving as the only viable fallback for IV (64.7% reduction)\"。\n\n这段文字只提了正面（IV 64.7%），没提 SIMEX 实际恶化了 front-door（-55.7%）和 exposure（-3.8%）。appendix Table 3 和 Algorithm 1 都正确处理了这个区分，但正文段落可能误导审稿人以为 SIMEX 普遍有效。建议加一句：\"MC-SIMEX is harmful for front-door and exposure topologies; gold labels are required for these.\"\n\n---\n\n**[severity: warning] Proposition 1 的条件在 method.tex 中过度简化**\n\nmethod.tex L57-58 写 \"provided the true effect and the omitted-variable bias share the same sign\"。但 appendix 的条件汇总表（Table 6）显示各拓扑条件不同：\n- Confounding/Mediation/Front-door: sign alignment 条件 ✓\n- Collider: |τ| > B̄_col（大小条件，非符号条件）\n- M-bias: \"Bias is attenuating\"（结构性质）\n\n主文用一个统一条件概括 5 个不同条件，可能被审稿人追问。建议 Proposition 1 中加 \"(see Appendix Table 6 for topology-specific conditions)\"。\n\n---\n\n### 上轮 Warning 修复确认\n\n1. **em-dash AI 风格** → ✅ 已清理。主文无 Unicode em-dash，仅剩 LaTeX `---` 用于合法场景（experiments.tex L115 的 appositive、appendix proposition 标题）。\n2. **Contribution 3 binarization 过载** → ✅ binarization 已移至 Contribution 1 末句，Contribution 3 专注 correction + FWL invariance，结构更合理。\n\n### 其他确认（info 级）\n\n- [severity: info] Table 1 caption \"1,000\" 已更正为 \"100,000\"，与 registry plan001 100k random matrices 一致 ✓\n- [severity: info] IV 段落删除了 \"(plan002 cross-K validation)\" 内部引用 ✓\n- [severity: info] Abstract/Intro 已精确标注 proof 覆盖范围：\"closed-form for confounding, exposure, and mediation; numerically validated for all seven\" + \"formal proofs for three topologies and Monte Carlo validation for all seven\" ✓\n- [severity: info] Appendix 新增 collider + front-door 完整证明（未 committed），proof 结构合理——collider 用 Cauchy-Schwarz bound，front-door 分解为 constant confounding + mediation-type leakage\n- [severity: info] Appendix Table 6 (sign_conditions) 是重要贡献——清晰列出每个拓扑的 sufficient condition + paper DGP 验证结果\n- [severity: info] aaai2026.sty 用于 AAAI 2027 投稿——仍待 AAAI 2027 模板发布后切换\n\n### 建议优先级\n\n1. **立即** commit working tree 变更（修复 Table 3 数据错误 + 消除 duplicate label）\n2. **下轮** 补充 §4.7 SIMEX 对 front-door/exposure 的负面表现披露\n3. **下轮** Prop 1 加 appendix 交叉引用",

- HumAID (K=10) - already had data
- VAST (K=3) - now has data from qwen2.5-7b + frontier models
- civil_comments (K=2) - now has data from qwen2.5-7b

Key findings:
1. K=3 (VAST) has the highest mean bias (65.3%), NOT K=2 or K=10


2. The IV method at K=3 pushes bias to 252%, with zero sign flips across all K values—critical for the sign-flip guarantee
3. Overall, 92 out of 210 scenarios are ASV-unsafe (43.8%)
4. I'm noticing the civil_comments dataset relies on qwen2.5-7b, which contradicts my earlier warning about not mixing 7B models with frontier models in the same empirical comparison

I need to verify whether the 7B and frontier model results are properly separated, and check if the paper is incorrectly claiming these as frontier LLM results. Scanning back through the registry entries I saw earlier, plan_006 doesn't appear at all among the 13 experiments listed.

The ROAD MAP does reference plan_006 as partially complete, but there's no corresponding registry entry for it, which is problematic since the paper cites plan_006 results in section 4.6. I should flag this discrepancy and verify the actual data on the server.

Critical observations from the data:

1. **civil_comments frontier models have ALL-ZERO confusion matrices!** llama4, qwen3, deepseek — all have [[0,0],[0,0]] for civil_comments. Only qwen2.5-7b has valid data.

2. **VAST: qwen3 has ALL-ZERO confusion matrices** (all 3 prompts). Only llama4 and deepseek + qwen2.5-7b have data.

This means:
- civil_comments: ONLY qwen2.5-7b data (3 configs out of 9, the rest are zeros from API 402)


- vast: llama4 and deepseek likely have some valid configs, plus qwen2.5-7b — maybe 6-9 total
- humaid: 8/9 as before

The worker claims "30 configs × 7 topologies = 210 scenarios" but zero confusion matrices aren't valid data — they indicate failed API calls, not actual results. So the analysis would be inflating the scenario count by treating these failures as legitimate data points. Looking at the civil_comments F1 scores reported for qwen2.5-7b (0.717–0.746), those are the only real results we have for that dataset.

Examining the actual confusion matrices, civil_comments shows reasonable 2×2 binary classification performance across three qwen2.5-7b variants, with diagonal values ranging from 0.679 to 0.748. But VAST's 3-class problem reveals a critical issue: the third class (likely "neutral" stance) has diagonal values as low as 0.046 for llama4, which is essentially random guessing. This confirms the handoff warning about near-random performance on VAST. So the real picture is much smaller than 30 configs — civil_comments has only 3 usable configs, VAST has a handful of valid ones, and humaid has 8 usable configs. I need to dig into the actual JSON data to figure out which configs are being counted as valid and understand how the 30 configs breaks down across these datasets.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
I can see all 30 confusion matrix keys in the JSON:
- civil_comments: 9 configs (llama4×3, qwen3×1, deepseek×2, qwen2.5-7b×3) — but I know frontier models have zero matrices
- vast: 12 configs (llama4×3, qwen3×3, deepseek×3, qwen2.5-7b×3) — qwen3 has zero matrices
- humaid: 9 configs (llama4×3, qwen3×3, deepseek×3) — 8 usable, 1 failed


Now I'm checking which ones actually have valid matrices. The frontier models in civil_comments are all empty, qwen3 across vast is empty, but the other model variants should have data I can work with. The worker result claims 210 valid scenarios, but if 9 configs are actually zeros, that's only 21 valid configs—meaning the stats could be heavily skewed by including those empty matrices in the calculations. The bias percentages for civil_comments and vast might be artificially deflated if zeros are being averaged in with real data.

Looking at the notation more carefully, the "0/12" and "mbias" labels suggest the worker is tracking 12 configs per dataset (4 models × 3 prompts), which would include those problematic zero entries from qwen3. This ambiguity about whether zeros are filtered out or counted as valid scenarios is making it hard to trust the reported percentages.

The real issue is that zero confusion matrices shouldn't produce meaningful bias statistics at all—the mathematical operations required for bias computation need non-degenerate data. I'm wondering if the script is silently skipping these zero cases or if it's somehow computing bias on empty data, which would be a fundamental methodological flaw.

Looking at the actual numbers more carefully, the "2/9" notation for confounding/mbias seems to indicate 2 out of 9 configs are unsafe, but if 6 of those 9 are actually zero matrices, then the denominator is misleading—it should really be 2 out of 3 valid configs.

The core problem is whether zero confusion matrices count as valid data points. If they do, it artificially inflates safety rates and deflates unsafe percentages across civil_comments and VAST. I need to flag that the "30 configs" claim includes these invalid entries, the ASV-unsafe percentages are likely diluted by padding with zeros, civil_comments K=2 relies exclusively on qwen2.5-7b rather than frontier models, and VAST K=3 mixes 7B with frontier data in a way that obscures which results come from which model class. I should also verify whether the paper's §4.6 already restricts analysis to humaid-only datasets or claims broader multi-dataset coverage.

The current paper only validates against HumAID, but plan_006 expands to include VAST and civil_comments, so these methodological issues become critical if the paper gets updated with the new results. Now I need to examine the VAST deepseek and qwen2.5-7b diagonal values more closely.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
Now I can see the JSON's summary section:
```json
"summary": {
    "f1_gt_093_total": 0,
    "f1_gt_093_with_high_bias_pct": 0.0,
    "ppi_recommended_scenarios": 92,
    "total_scenarios": 210,
    "ppi_recommended_pct": 43.81
}
```

So total_scenarios = 210 = 30 × 7. The worker is counting ALL 30 configs.

But I also notice a critical data quality issue in the inter-model correlation:


Looking at the VAST qwen3-deepseek comparisons, both few-shot-3 and few-shot-5 show agreement=1.0 with kappa=0.0, which means the models predicted identically on every sample but only because they both defaulted to the majority class. Since qwen3 has no confusion matrices for VAST, this suggests deepseek is either doing the same or the comparison logic is broken.

The real puzzle is how we're getting agreement=1.0 and error_correlation=1.0 when qwen3's VAST data appears to be all zeros. If both models had zero data, the correlation should return NaN, not 1.0. But looking closer, qwen3's zero-shot VAST confusion matrix is completely empty while deepseek's zero-shot actually contains data, so the correlation computation might be handling the empty case differently than I expected. The discrepancy between zero-shot and few-shot agreement scores is a red flag—if qwen3 has no predictions for VAST, these correlations are artifacts rather than meaningful signals. This reveals a fundamental data quality problem: the worker didn't filter out the all-zero confusion matrices from the 402 API failures, so at least nine configs are contaminating the inter-model correlation analysis with spurious results. I need to flag this to the Director as a critical issue undermining the validity of the 210 total scenarios reported. frontier model data. The "36.9% mean bias" and "30.2% ASV-unsafe" for K=2 is from a 7B model only — cannot be presented as equivalent to the humaid frontier results.

3. **Inter-model correlations are spurious for VAST**: qwen3-deepseek correlations in VAST are computed on zero data (qwen3 has no VAST predictions), producing meaningless values (1.0 agreement, 1.0 error correlation).

4. **VAST third-class accuracy extremely low**: llama4 VAST diagonal for class 3 is 0.046 (near zero), meaning the model essentially ignores the neutral/other category. This is a 3-class task where one class has ~5% recall.

5. **Registry not updated**: plan_006 has no entry in the experiment registry despite being a key empirical result.

6. **K=3 bias finding needs scrutiny**: The reported K=3 bias of 65.3% versus K=2's 36.9% and K=10's 12.2% might reflect differences in model quality across datasets rather than a genuine K effect, since different models were evaluated on different datasets.
